Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’m here because most days I wake up and it already feels like there’s this gray weight sitting on me. Like before anything’s even happened, I’m already low.\n\nI work landscaping, so I’m out with the crew, moving around, doing what needs doing, and then I go home and I’m around my kids, and… I don’t know, I still can’t shake it. It’s like I’m there, but not really there. Just kind of hollow, going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way most days? Can you pinpoint when these feelings started or if they’ve come and gone before?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I think. About three years and a few months. I can kind of trace it back to when the groundskeeping contract changed and our schedule got all messed up — longer days, earlier starts, like my whole life got swallowed by work.\n\nI mean, I remember thinking at first, “Okay, it’s just the busy season,” because in landscaping you get used to that, you know? Weather changes, clients want everything done yesterday, equipment breaks down at the worst time… but it just never really let up. Since then it’s been most days, just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when your mood was unusually high or you felt really energized, where you didn’t need as much sleep or felt like you could take on a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… unusually high? I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, there are days at work where you get moving and the sun’s out and the mower’s running right for once, and you kind of feel like, okay, maybe today won’t be terrible. But that’s not like… some big burst or anything.\n\nSleep’s been weird sometimes just because of work hours, you know, early starts, crews getting shuffled around, somebody calls out and suddenly you’re covering another property. Back when I was younger I could do that and still have energy to mess around after, but now it just catches up with me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had times where I felt on top of the world or like I didn’t need sleep. Not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks straight, where it felt different from just having a bad day or being stressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s more than a bad day. It’s like most mornings I wake up and before anything even happens, there’s already this gray weight on me.\n\nAnd I’ll go to work, be out there with the crew, doing lawns or hauling stuff or whatever the day is, and sometimes you’d think being busy would pull you out of your head, but it doesn’t really. Then I get home and I’m around my kids, and I know I should feel more present, but I just feel… low. Hollow, kind of. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to, not because I’m really in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like spending time with your kids or anything else you did for fun, don’t bring you the same pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess I haven’t really thought about it in that exact way.\n\nI mean, with the kids, I still like being around them, sure. They’ll tell me some story from school or want to show me something on a game, and I try to listen. Sometimes I get distracted, though — like I’ll be thinking about whether I left the trimmer in the truck or if tomorrow’s crew is going to have enough people, and then I realize I missed half of what they said.\n\nAs far as fun stuff… I don’t do a whole lot anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy it or just because life got kind of full and tiring. I used to mess around in the yard at home, fix things, watch games sometimes. Now if I sit down, half the time I’m just sitting there. But I couldn’t tell you if it’s no pleasure exactly, or just… I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard to separate, because fixing things used to just be what I did when something broke, you know? Like if the mower blade needed sharpening or a gate hinge was loose, I’d mess with it and feel like, alright, at least that’s handled.\n\nNow half the time if I start something, I’m thinking about three other things—like whether the truck needs gas, or if my kid has something for school, or if rain’s gonna mess up the route tomorrow. And games… I’ll put one on and then realize I haven’t really been watching, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy it or I’m just tired or distracted.\n\nSo I guess I can’t really say for sure it’s not pleasurable. It’s more like I don’t pay attention to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to answer that. Like with fixing things, sometimes I still feel okay once it’s done, like, “good, that’s off the list.” But is that pleasure? I don’t know.\n\nAnd watching games… I mean, I used to follow the whole season more, knew who was injured, who was getting traded, all that. My brother-in-law still texts me about it sometimes, and I’ll realize I don’t even know what he’s talking about. But then again, everybody’s busy, right? Work runs long, there’s stuff at home, the truck needs something, kids need rides… by the time I sit down, I’m half somewhere else anyway.\n\nSo yeah, maybe they don’t feel the same, but I can’t say for sure. It’s more like I don’t really settle into anything long enough to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take any real interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you have the time to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no longer take any real interest.” That sounds kind of… final, I guess.\n\nIf I have time, which isn’t that often, I’ll still put something on or tinker with something if it needs doing. Like the other day I was looking for a socket wrench and ended up cleaning out this whole drawer in the garage because there were nails and sprinkler parts and old receipts all mixed together. My wife’s been after me about that drawer forever. And then by the time I found what I needed, I didn’t even do the thing I started out to do.\n\nSo… I mean, I don’t know. Maybe I don’t get into things the same way, but I haven’t really sat there and thought, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.” It’s more like I’m doing one thing and my head’s already on the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Even when you do have some free time, do you find that you don’t really get much pleasure or interest out of things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, but I don’t know if I can pin it down like that. If I get free time, it usually turns into catching up on something else anyway. Like I’ll sit down for a game and then remember the weed trimmer line is almost gone, or there’s that one section by the fence that’s been bugging me, and then I’m up looking for something in the shed.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s that I don’t get pleasure out of it, or I just don’t really stay with it long enough to notice. Sometimes fixing something feels good just because it’s done, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking about enjoying it. And games… half the time they’re just kind of on.\n\nDoctor: When you do have a moment where nothing else is pressing—no work, no family obligations, nothing urgent—do you still find yourself not really interested or getting pleasure from the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games?\n\nPatient: I mean… if literally nothing’s pressing, I guess that hardly ever happens, you know? There’s always something. Even if it’s not urgent, I’ll notice the hose isn’t rolled right or there’s mud on my boots or I should check the mower blades before Monday.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I sit there with a game on and feel like, “yeah, this is great,” I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just watching the clock or flipping around. And with fixing things, I’ll start out thinking I’m going to enjoy having a little project, then I end up sorting screws by size or looking at some cracked plastic bin I’ve been meaning to replace. My dad used to keep coffee cans full of bolts, all labeled with masking tape, and I always thought that was overkill, but now I kind of get it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clean answer. I don’t notice much “pleasure,” but I also don’t know if that’s because I’m not interested or because my mind just doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you no longer care about them or feel any interest in them, even while you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care.” That feels too strong. I mean, if something’s broken, I care enough to fix it, or at least I care that it’s sitting there broken. Like if a gate latch is sticking, I’ll mess with it because every time I walk by it catches my eye.\n\nBut while I’m doing it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m thinking about the next thing more than the thing itself. Same with a game, I might have it on and I’m aware of the score, but then I’m noticing the coffee table’s got stuff piled on it, or I should’ve brought the cooler in from the truck, and then I’m up doing that.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure what that counts as. I wouldn’t say I feel nothing about it. I just don’t really sit there and think, “I’m enjoying this,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Even when you have the chance to do something you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t really care about doing it anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “don’t care anymore.” I still… I mean, if the game’s on, I’ll turn it on sometimes. Or if there’s something to fix, I’ll mess with it. But whether I’m doing it because I care about it, or because it’s just there and needs doing, I don’t really know.\n\nLike last weekend I had the TV on, and I was kind of watching, but then I noticed one of the blinds was crooked, and that turned into looking for the little plastic piece for it, and then I was in the drawer with all the batteries and tape and old keys—every house has that drawer, I think. So by the time I came back, I’d missed half the game.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clean “I don’t care” feeling. It’s more like I don’t stay with it long enough to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you do the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or interest in them anymore — like they just feel flat or empty?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “flat or empty” sounds kind of dramatic, maybe. I mean, sometimes it is just on in the background, like the game is on and I’m not really pulled into it, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because it feels empty or because I’m thinking about something else.\n\nWith fixing things, it’s not like I pick up a wrench and feel nothing exactly. It’s more like I’m looking at the hinge, then I notice the screw heads are stripped, then I’m thinking I should reorganize that toolbox because the smaller socket set keeps ending up under the rags. I had this old neighbor who kept everything on pegboard, outlines drawn around the tools like a crime scene, and I used to laugh at that, but honestly it probably saves time.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as “no pleasure.” I just don’t always stay with the thing long enough to know what I’m feeling about it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about other tasks or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less pleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I really had nothing else in my head, maybe I could tell you better, but that almost never seems to happen. There’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or something I forgot.\n\nLike with a game, even if I sit down on purpose, I’ll start noticing the floor by the door needs sweeping, or I’ll remember I left a pair of work gloves in the truck and if it rains they’ll get ruined. Then I’m up, and by the time I sit back down, I don’t know if the game wasn’t interesting or if I just wandered off from it.\n\nFixing things is kind of the same. Sometimes I get into it a little, but then it turns into three other things. I’ll go looking for one screwdriver and end up sorting a coffee can full of screws because none of them match anything. So… I don’t know that I can say it feels less pleasurable exactly. I just don’t really get a clean moment where I’m only doing that one thing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work, family, or other responsibilities, do you notice that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less enjoyable or less meaningful to you emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m having a hard time separating it out like that. I mean, if I wasn’t thinking about work or family or whatever… I don’t know when that is, honestly.\n\nLike with a game, I can sit there and maybe there’s a decent play and I’ll notice it, but then I’m thinking the announcer sounds like that guy who used to do the local radio ads for the tire place — which makes me think I need to check the tread on my truck tires — and then I’m not really with the game anymore.\n\nSo is it less enjoyable? Maybe. Or maybe I just don’t stay put in it long enough to feel much one way or the other. “Meaningful emotionally” is kind of hard for me to answer. I haven’t really sat there and thought, “This used to mean something and now it doesn’t.” It’s more like it comes and goes and I lose track of it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… I still don’t know how to answer it cleanly. If I’m not distracted, maybe I’d notice, but I don’t really catch myself in that kind of quiet space much.\n\nLike, watching a game, I’ll think I’m settled in, and then I’m wondering why they changed the uniforms, or I remember my cousin had a jacket in those same colors back when we were kids, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of the game. Sometimes I get distracted, though — like I’ll be thinking about whether I left the trimmer in the truck or if tomorrow’s crew is going to have enough people, and then I realize I missed half of what they said.\n\nAs far as fun stuff… I don’t do a whole lot anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy it or just because life got kind of full and tiring. I used to mess around in the yard at home, fix things, watch games sometimes. Now if I sit down, half the time I’m just sitting there. But I couldn’t tell you if it’s no pleasure exactly, or just… I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard to separate, because fixing things used to just be what I did when something broke, you know? Like if the mower blade needed sharpening or a gate hinge was loose, I’d mess with it and feel like, alright, at least that’s handled.\n\nNow half the time if I start something, I’m thinking about three other things—like whether the truck needs gas, or if my kid has something for school, or if rain’s gonna mess up the route tomorrow. And games… I’ll put one on and then realize I haven’t really been watching, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy it or I’m just tired or distracted.\n\nSo I guess I can’t really say for sure it’s not pleasurable. It’s more like I don’t pay attention to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to answer that. Like with fixing things, sometimes I still feel okay once it’s done, like, “good, that’s off the list.” But is that pleasure? I don’t know.\n\nAnd watching games… I mean, I used to follow the whole season more, knew who was injured, who was getting traded, all that. My brother-in-law still texts me about it sometimes, and I’ll realize I don’t even know what he’s talking about. But then again, everybody’s busy, right? Work runs long, there’s stuff at home, the truck needs something, kids need rides… by the time I sit down, I’m half somewhere else anyway.\n\nSo yeah, maybe they don’t feel the same, but I can’t say for sure. It’s more like I don’t really settle into anything long enough to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take any real interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you have the time to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no longer take any real interest.” That sounds kind of… final, I guess.\n\nIf I have time, which isn’t that often, I’ll still put something on or tinker with something if it needs doing. Like the other day I was looking for a socket wrench and ended up cleaning out this whole drawer in the garage because there were nails and sprinkler parts and old receipts all mixed together. My wife’s been after me about that drawer forever. And then by the time I found what I needed, I didn’t even do the thing I started out to do.\n\nSo… I mean, I don’t know. Maybe I don’t get into things the same way, but I haven’t really sat there and thought, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.” It’s more like I’m doing one thing and my head’s already on the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Even when you do have some free time, do you find that you don’t really get much pleasure or interest out of things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, but I don’t know if I can pin it down like that. If I get free time, it usually turns into catching up on something else anyway. Like I’ll sit down for a game and then remember the weed trimmer line is almost gone, or there’s that one section by the fence that’s been bugging me, and then I’m up looking for something in the shed.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s that I don’t get pleasure out of it, or I just don’t really stay with it long enough to notice. Sometimes fixing something feels good just because it’s done, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking about enjoying it. And games… half the time they’re just kind of on.\n\nDoctor: When you do have a moment where nothing else is pressing—no work, no family obligations, nothing urgent—do you still find yourself not really interested or getting pleasure from the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games?\n\nPatient: I mean… if literally nothing’s pressing, I guess that hardly ever happens, you know? There’s always something. Even if it’s not urgent, I’ll notice the hose isn’t rolled right or there’s mud on my boots or I should check the mower blades before Monday.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I sit there with a game on and feel like, “yeah, this is great,” I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just watching the clock or flipping around. And with fixing things, I’ll start out thinking I’m going to enjoy having a little project, then I end up sorting screws by size or looking at some cracked plastic bin I’ve been meaning to replace. My dad used to keep coffee cans full of bolts, all labeled with masking tape, and I always thought that was overkill, but now I kind of get it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clean answer. I don’t notice much “pleasure,” but I also don’t know if that’s because I’m not interested or because my mind just doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you no longer care about them or feel any interest in them, even while you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care.” That feels too strong. I mean, if something’s broken, I care enough to fix it, or at least I care that it’s sitting there broken. Like if a gate latch is sticking, I’ll mess with it because every time I walk by it catches my eye.\n\nBut while I’m doing it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m thinking about the next thing more than the thing itself. Same with a game, I might have it on and I’m aware of the score, but then I’m noticing the coffee table’s got stuff piled on it, or I should’ve brought the cooler in from the truck, and then I’m up doing that.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure what that counts as. I wouldn’t say I feel nothing about it. I just don’t really sit there and think, “I’m enjoying this,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Even when you have the chance to do something you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t really care about doing it anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “don’t care anymore.” I still… I mean, if the game’s on, I’ll turn it on sometimes. Or if there’s something to fix, I’ll mess with it. But whether I’m doing it because I care about it, or because it’s just there and needs doing, I don’t really know.\n\nLike last weekend I had the TV on, and I was kind of watching, but then I noticed one of the blinds was crooked, and that turned into looking for the little plastic piece for it, and then I was in the drawer with all the batteries and tape and old keys—every house has that drawer, I think. So by the time I came back, I’d missed half the game.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clean “I don’t care” feeling. It’s more like I don’t stay with it long enough to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you do the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or interest in them anymore — like they just feel flat or empty?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “flat or empty” sounds kind of dramatic, maybe. I mean, sometimes it is just on in the background, like the game is on and I’m not really pulled into it, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because it feels empty or because I’m thinking about something else.\n\nWith fixing things, it’s not like I pick up a wrench and feel nothing exactly. It’s more like I’m looking at the hinge, then I notice the screw heads are stripped, then I’m thinking I should reorganize that toolbox because the smaller socket set keeps ending up under the rags. I had this old neighbor who kept everything on pegboard, outlines drawn around the tools like a crime scene, and I used to laugh at that, but honestly it probably saves time.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as “no pleasure.” I just don’t always stay with the thing long enough to know what I’m feeling about it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about other tasks or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less pleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I really had nothing else in my head, maybe I could tell you better, but that almost never seems to happen. There’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or something I forgot.\n\nLike with a game, even if I sit down on purpose, I’ll start noticing the floor by the door needs sweeping, or I’ll remember I left a pair of work gloves in the truck and if it rains they’ll get ruined. Then I’m up, and by the time I sit back down, I don’t know if the game wasn’t interesting or if I just wandered off from it.\n\nFixing things is kind of the same. Sometimes I get into it a little, but then it turns into three other things. I’ll go looking for one screwdriver and end up sorting a coffee can full of screws because none of them match anything. So… I don’t know that I can say it feels less pleasurable exactly. I just don’t really get a clean moment where I’m only doing that one thing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work, family, or other responsibilities, do you notice that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less enjoyable or less meaningful to you emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m having a hard time separating it out like that. I mean, if I wasn’t thinking about work or family or whatever… I don’t know when that is, honestly.\n\nLike with a game, I can sit there and maybe there’s a decent play and I’ll notice it, but then I’m thinking the announcer sounds like that guy who used to do the local radio ads for the tire place — which makes me think I need to check the tread on my truck tires — and then I’m not really with the game anymore.\n\nSo is it less enjoyable? Maybe. Or maybe I just don’t stay put in it long enough to feel much one way or the other. “Meaningful emotionally” is kind of hard for me to answer. I haven’t really sat there and thought, “This used to mean something and now it doesn’t.” It’s more like it comes and goes and I lose track of it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… I still don’t know how to answer it cleanly. If I’m not distracted, maybe I’d notice, but I don’t really catch myself in that kind of quiet space much.\n\nLike, watching a game, I’ll think I’m settled in, and then I’m wondering why they changed the uniforms, or I remember my cousin had a jacket in those same colors back when we were kids, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of the game. So I can’t say, “Yeah, it’s not enjoyable anymore.” It’s more like I lose the thread.\n\nWith fixing something, sometimes I still like figuring out what’s wrong, but then it turns into hunting for the right part or noticing three other things that need doing. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it as less enjoyable exactly.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less enjoyable or less meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can really say it that way. I mean, if I’m sitting with a game on, sometimes I’ll still look up when there’s a good play, or I’ll think, “That was something.” But then I start wondering where one of the players went to college, and that reminds me of a guy I worked with years ago who was always talking about college football, and then I’m not really watching anymore.\n\nSo… less meaningful, I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer for it. Fixing things, sometimes I’ll still get interested if I can figure out what’s wrong with it, but it doesn’t stay simple. I’ll go out to the shed for a wrench and notice the hose is kinked or there’s a bag of mulch I never moved, and then the whole thing turns into something else.\n\nI guess I haven’t sat there and thought, “I don’t enjoy this now.” It’s more like I don’t get a clear enough stretch with it to know.\n\nDoctor: When you're not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out like that. I mean, if I’m not distracted… I don’t know, that’s the part I don’t really get much of.\n\nLike with fixing something, sometimes I’ll start in on it and for a minute I’m focused, but then I’m thinking about where I put the socket set, and then I remember the shelf in the garage is sagging, and then I’m looking at that instead. So I can’t tell if the thing itself is less interesting, or if I just don’t stay with it.\n\nSame with a game. I might have it on, but I’m half watching, half thinking about something else. Like before anything’s even happened, I’m already low.\n\nI work landscaping, so I’m out with the crew, moving around, doing what needs doing, and then I go home and I’m around my kids, and… I don’t know, I still can’t shake it. It’s like I’m there, but not really there. Just kind of hollow, going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way most days? Can you pinpoint when these feelings started or if they’ve come and gone before?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years, I think. About three years and a few months. I can kind of trace it back to when the groundskeeping contract changed and our schedule got all messed up — longer days, earlier starts, like my whole life got swallowed by work.\n\nI mean, I remember thinking at first, “Okay, it’s just the busy season,” because in landscaping you get used to that, you know? Weather changes, clients want everything done yesterday, equipment breaks down at the worst time… but it just never really let up. Since then it’s been most days, just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when your mood was unusually high or you felt really energized, where you didn’t need as much sleep or felt like you could take on a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… unusually high? I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, there are days at work where you get moving and the sun’s out and the mower’s running right for once, and you kind of feel like, okay, maybe today won’t be terrible. But that’s not like… some big burst or anything.\n\nSleep’s been weird sometimes just because of work hours, you know, early starts, crews getting shuffled around, somebody calls out and suddenly you’re covering another property. Back when I was younger I could do that and still have energy to mess around after, but now it just catches up with me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had times where I felt on top of the world or like I didn’t need sleep. Not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks straight, where it felt different from just having a bad day or being stressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s more than a bad day. It’s like most mornings I wake up and before anything even happens, there’s already this gray weight on me.\n\nAnd I’ll go to work, be out there with the crew, doing lawns or hauling stuff or whatever the day is, and sometimes you’d think being busy would pull you out of your head, but it doesn’t really. Then I get home and I’m around my kids, and I know I should feel more present, but I just feel… low. Hollow, kind of. Like I’m moving through the day because I have to, not because I’m really in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like spending time with your kids or anything else you did for fun, don’t bring you the same pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I guess I haven’t really thought about it in that exact way.\n\nI mean, with the kids, I still like being around them, sure. They’ll tell me some story from school or want to show me something on a game, and I try to listen. Sometimes I get distracted, though — like I’ll be thinking about whether I left the trimmer in the truck or if tomorrow’s crew is going to have enough people, and then I realize I missed half of what they said.\n\nAs far as fun stuff… I don’t do a whole lot anymore, but I don’t know if that’s because I don’t enjoy it or just because life got kind of full and tiring. I used to mess around in the yard at home, fix things, watch games sometimes. Now if I sit down, half the time I’m just sitting there. But I couldn’t tell you if it’s no pleasure exactly, or just… I’m worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… maybe? It’s hard to separate, because fixing things used to just be what I did when something broke, you know? Like if the mower blade needed sharpening or a gate hinge was loose, I’d mess with it and feel like, alright, at least that’s handled.\n\nNow half the time if I start something, I’m thinking about three other things—like whether the truck needs gas, or if my kid has something for school, or if rain’s gonna mess up the route tomorrow. And games… I’ll put one on and then realize I haven’t really been watching, but I don’t know if that means I don’t enjoy it or I’m just tired or distracted.\n\nSo I guess I can’t really say for sure it’s not pleasurable. It’s more like I don’t pay attention to it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, don’t feel as satisfying or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not totally sure how to answer that. Like with fixing things, sometimes I still feel okay once it’s done, like, “good, that’s off the list.” But is that pleasure? I don’t know.\n\nAnd watching games… I mean, I used to follow the whole season more, knew who was injured, who was getting traded, all that. My brother-in-law still texts me about it sometimes, and I’ll realize I don’t even know what he’s talking about. But then again, everybody’s busy, right? Work runs long, there’s stuff at home, the truck needs something, kids need rides… by the time I sit down, I’m half somewhere else anyway.\n\nSo yeah, maybe they don’t feel the same, but I can’t say for sure. It’s more like I don’t really settle into anything long enough to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take any real interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, even when you have the time to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “no longer take any real interest.” That sounds kind of… final, I guess.\n\nIf I have time, which isn’t that often, I’ll still put something on or tinker with something if it needs doing. Like the other day I was looking for a socket wrench and ended up cleaning out this whole drawer in the garage because there were nails and sprinkler parts and old receipts all mixed together. My wife’s been after me about that drawer forever. And then by the time I found what I needed, I didn’t even do the thing I started out to do.\n\nSo… I mean, I don’t know. Maybe I don’t get into things the same way, but I haven’t really sat there and thought, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.” It’s more like I’m doing one thing and my head’s already on the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Even when you do have some free time, do you find that you don’t really get much pleasure or interest out of things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, but I don’t know if I can pin it down like that. If I get free time, it usually turns into catching up on something else anyway. Like I’ll sit down for a game and then remember the weed trimmer line is almost gone, or there’s that one section by the fence that’s been bugging me, and then I’m up looking for something in the shed.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s that I don’t get pleasure out of it, or I just don’t really stay with it long enough to notice. Sometimes fixing something feels good just because it’s done, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking about enjoying it. And games… half the time they’re just kind of on.\n\nDoctor: When you do have a moment where nothing else is pressing—no work, no family obligations, nothing urgent—do you still find yourself not really interested or getting pleasure from the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games?\n\nPatient: I mean… if literally nothing’s pressing, I guess that hardly ever happens, you know? There’s always something. Even if it’s not urgent, I’ll notice the hose isn’t rolled right or there’s mud on my boots or I should check the mower blades before Monday.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I sit there with a game on and feel like, “yeah, this is great,” I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just watching the clock or flipping around. And with fixing things, I’ll start out thinking I’m going to enjoy having a little project, then I end up sorting screws by size or looking at some cracked plastic bin I’ve been meaning to replace. My dad used to keep coffee cans full of bolts, all labeled with masking tape, and I always thought that was overkill, but now I kind of get it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clean answer. I don’t notice much “pleasure,” but I also don’t know if that’s because I’m not interested or because my mind just doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you no longer care about them or feel any interest in them, even while you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care.” That feels too strong. I mean, if something’s broken, I care enough to fix it, or at least I care that it’s sitting there broken. Like if a gate latch is sticking, I’ll mess with it because every time I walk by it catches my eye.\n\nBut while I’m doing it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m thinking about the next thing more than the thing itself. Same with a game, I might have it on and I’m aware of the score, but then I’m noticing the coffee table’s got stuff piled on it, or I should’ve brought the cooler in from the truck, and then I’m up doing that.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure what that counts as. I wouldn’t say I feel nothing about it. I just don’t really sit there and think, “I’m enjoying this,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Even when you have the chance to do something you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t really care about doing it anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “don’t care anymore.” I still… I mean, if the game’s on, I’ll turn it on sometimes. Or if there’s something to fix, I’ll mess with it. But whether I’m doing it because I care about it, or because it’s just there and needs doing, I don’t really know.\n\nLike last weekend I had the TV on, and I was kind of watching, but then I noticed one of the blinds was crooked, and that turned into looking for the little plastic piece for it, and then I was in the drawer with all the batteries and tape and old keys—every house has that drawer, I think. So by the time I came back, I’d missed half the game.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clean “I don’t care” feeling. It’s more like I don’t stay with it long enough to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you do the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or interest in them anymore — like they just feel flat or empty?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “flat or empty” sounds kind of dramatic, maybe. I mean, sometimes it is just on in the background, like the game is on and I’m not really pulled into it, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because it feels empty or because I’m thinking about something else.\n\nWith fixing things, it’s not like I pick up a wrench and feel nothing exactly. It’s more like I’m looking at the hinge, then I notice the screw heads are stripped, then I’m thinking I should reorganize that toolbox because the smaller socket set keeps ending up under the rags. I had this old neighbor who kept everything on pegboard, outlines drawn around the tools like a crime scene, and I used to laugh at that, but honestly it probably saves time.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as “no pleasure.” I just don’t always stay with the thing long enough to know what I’m feeling about it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about other tasks or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less pleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I really had nothing else in my head, maybe I could tell you better, but that almost never seems to happen. There’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or something I forgot.\n\nLike with a game, even if I sit down on purpose, I’ll start noticing the floor by the door needs sweeping, or I’ll remember I left a pair of work gloves in the truck and if it rains they’ll get ruined. Then I’m up, and by the time I sit back down, I don’t know if the game wasn’t interesting or if I just wandered off from it.\n\nFixing things is kind of the same. Sometimes I get into it a little, but then it turns into three other things. I’ll go looking for one screwdriver and end up sorting a coffee can full of screws because none of them match anything. So… I don’t know that I can say it feels less pleasurable exactly. I just don’t really get a clean moment where I’m only doing that one thing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work, family, or other responsibilities, do you notice that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less enjoyable or less meaningful to you emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m having a hard time separating it out like that. I mean, if I wasn’t thinking about work or family or whatever… I don’t know when that is, honestly.\n\nLike with a game, I can sit there and maybe there’s a decent play and I’ll notice it, but then I’m thinking the announcer sounds like that guy who used to do the local radio ads for the tire place — which makes me think I need to check the tread on my truck tires — and then I’m not really with the game anymore.\n\nSo is it less enjoyable? Maybe. Or maybe I just don’t stay put in it long enough to feel much one way or the other. “Meaningful emotionally” is kind of hard for me to answer. I haven’t really sat there and thought, “This used to mean something and now it doesn’t.” It’s more like it comes and goes and I lose track of it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… I still don’t know how to answer it cleanly. If I’m not distracted, maybe I’d notice, but I don’t really catch myself in that kind of quiet space much.\n\nLike, watching a game, I’ll think I’m settled in, and then I’m wondering why they changed the uniforms, or I remember my cousin had a jacket in those same colors back when we were kids, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of the game. So I can’t say, “Yeah, it’s not enjoyable anymore.” It’s more like I lose the thread.\n\nWith fixing something, sometimes I still like figuring out what’s wrong, but then it turns into hunting for the right part or noticing three other things that need doing. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it as less enjoyable exactly.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less enjoyable or less meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can really say it that way. I mean, if I’m sitting with a game on, sometimes I’ll still look up when there’s a good play, or I’ll think, “That was something.” But then I start wondering where one of the players went to college, and that reminds me of a guy I worked with years ago who was always talking about college football, and then I’m not really watching anymore.\n\nSo… less meaningful, I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer for it. Fixing things, sometimes I’ll still get interested if I can figure out what’s wrong with it, but it doesn’t stay simple. I’ll go out to the shed for a wrench and notice the hose is kinked or there’s a bag of mulch I never moved, and then the whole thing turns into something else.\n\nI guess I haven’t sat there and thought, “I don’t enjoy this now.” It’s more like I don’t get a clear enough stretch with it to know.\n\nDoctor: When you're not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out like that. I mean, if I’m not distracted… I don’t know, that’s the part I don’t really get much of.\n\nLike with fixing something, sometimes I’ll start in on it and for a minute I’m focused, but then I’m thinking about where I put the socket set, and then I remember the shelf in the garage is sagging, and then I’m looking at that instead. So I can’t tell if the thing itself is less interesting, or if I just don’t stay with it.\n\nSame with a game. I might have it on, but I’m half watching, half thinking about something else. I don’t really sit there and think, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.” It’s just… kind of hard for me to say one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about work, family, or other tasks, do you notice that activities like fixing things or watching games feel less engaging or less interesting to you than they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can pin it down, honestly. If I’m really not thinking about work or family or the stuff waiting on me, I don’t know that I get enough of that kind of clear space to compare it.\n\nLike a game might be on, and I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck, or some little thing reminds me of a job site from years ago, and then I’m off on that. With fixing things, I can still get pulled in for a minute if I’m trying to figure out what’s wrong, but then it branches off into other stuff.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t noticed it as “less interesting” exactly. It’s more that I don’t stay with it long enough to know.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about work, family, or other responsibilities, do you notice that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less pleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I hear what you’re asking, I just… I don’t know if I can give you a yes-or-no on it. And with fixing things, I’ll start out thinking I’m going to enjoy having a little project, then I end up sorting screws by size or looking at some cracked plastic bin I’ve been meaning to replace. My dad used to keep coffee cans full of bolts, all labeled with masking tape, and I always thought that was overkill, but now I kind of get it.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t really have a clean answer. I don’t notice much “pleasure,” but I also don’t know if that’s because I’m not interested or because my mind just doesn’t stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you no longer care about them or feel any interest in them, even while you’re doing them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “no longer care.” That feels too strong. I mean, if something’s broken, I care enough to fix it, or at least I care that it’s sitting there broken. Like if a gate latch is sticking, I’ll mess with it because every time I walk by it catches my eye.\n\nBut while I’m doing it… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m thinking about the next thing more than the thing itself. Same with a game, I might have it on and I’m aware of the score, but then I’m noticing the coffee table’s got stuff piled on it, or I should’ve brought the cooler in from the truck, and then I’m up doing that.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure what that counts as. I wouldn’t say I feel nothing about it. I just don’t really sit there and think, “I’m enjoying this,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Even when you have the chance to do something you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t really care about doing it anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “don’t care anymore.” I still… I mean, if the game’s on, I’ll turn it on sometimes. Or if there’s something to fix, I’ll mess with it. But whether I’m doing it because I care about it, or because it’s just there and needs doing, I don’t really know.\n\nLike last weekend I had the TV on, and I was kind of watching, but then I noticed one of the blinds was crooked, and that turned into looking for the little plastic piece for it, and then I was in the drawer with all the batteries and tape and old keys—every house has that drawer, I think. So by the time I came back, I’d missed half the game.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clean “I don’t care” feeling. It’s more like I don’t stay with it long enough to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you do the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or interest in them anymore — like they just feel flat or empty?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “flat or empty” sounds kind of dramatic, maybe. I mean, sometimes it is just on in the background, like the game is on and I’m not really pulled into it, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s because it feels empty or because I’m thinking about something else.\n\nWith fixing things, it’s not like I pick up a wrench and feel nothing exactly. It’s more like I’m looking at the hinge, then I notice the screw heads are stripped, then I’m thinking I should reorganize that toolbox because the smaller socket set keeps ending up under the rags. I had this old neighbor who kept everything on pegboard, outlines drawn around the tools like a crime scene, and I used to laugh at that, but honestly it probably saves time.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it as “no pleasure.” I just don’t always stay with the thing long enough to know what I’m feeling about it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about other tasks or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less pleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. If I really had nothing else in my head, maybe I could tell you better, but that almost never seems to happen. There’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or something I forgot.\n\nLike with a game, even if I sit down on purpose, I’ll start noticing the floor by the door needs sweeping, or I’ll remember I left a pair of work gloves in the truck and if it rains they’ll get ruined. Then I’m up, and by the time I sit back down, I don’t know if the game wasn’t interesting or if I just wandered off from it.\n\nFixing things is kind of the same. Sometimes I get into it a little, but then it turns into three other things. I’ll go looking for one screwdriver and end up sorting a coffee can full of screws because none of them match anything. So… I don’t know that I can say it feels less pleasurable exactly. I just don’t really get a clean moment where I’m only doing that one thing.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about work, family, or other responsibilities, do you notice that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less enjoyable or less meaningful to you emotionally?\n\nPatient: I’m having a hard time separating it out like that. I mean, if I wasn’t thinking about work or family or whatever… I don’t know when that is, honestly.\n\nLike with a game, I can sit there and maybe there’s a decent play and I’ll notice it, but then I’m thinking the announcer sounds like that guy who used to do the local radio ads for the tire place — which makes me think I need to check the tread on my truck tires — and then I’m not really with the game anymore.\n\nSo is it less enjoyable? Maybe. Or maybe I just don’t stay put in it long enough to feel much one way or the other. “Meaningful emotionally” is kind of hard for me to answer. I haven’t really sat there and thought, “This used to mean something and now it doesn’t.” It’s more like it comes and goes and I lose track of it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that the things you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I guess… I still don’t know how to answer it cleanly. If I’m not distracted, maybe I’d notice, but I don’t really catch myself in that kind of quiet space much.\n\nLike, watching a game, I’ll think I’m settled in, and then I’m wondering why they changed the uniforms, or I remember my cousin had a jacket in those same colors back when we were kids, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of the game. So I can’t say, “Yeah, it’s not enjoyable anymore.” It’s more like I lose the thread.\n\nWith fixing something, sometimes I still like figuring out what’s wrong, but then it turns into hunting for the right part or noticing three other things that need doing. So… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it as less enjoyable exactly.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less enjoyable or less meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can really say it that way. I mean, if I’m sitting with a game on, sometimes I’ll still look up when there’s a good play, or I’ll think, “That was something.” But then I start wondering where one of the players went to college, and that reminds me of a guy I worked with years ago who was always talking about college football, and then I’m not really watching anymore.\n\nSo… less meaningful, I don’t know. I’m not trying to dodge the question. I just don’t have a clean answer for it. Fixing things, sometimes I’ll still get interested if I can figure out what’s wrong with it, but it doesn’t stay simple. I’ll go out to the shed for a wrench and notice the hose is kinked or there’s a bag of mulch I never moved, and then the whole thing turns into something else.\n\nI guess I haven’t sat there and thought, “I don’t enjoy this now.” It’s more like I don’t get a clear enough stretch with it to know.\n\nDoctor: When you're not distracted by other thoughts or responsibilities, do you find that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less enjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out like that. I mean, if I’m not distracted… I don’t know, that’s the part I don’t really get much of.\n\nLike with fixing something, sometimes I’ll start in on it and for a minute I’m focused, but then I’m thinking about where I put the socket set, and then I remember the shelf in the garage is sagging, and then I’m looking at that instead. So I can’t tell if the thing itself is less interesting, or if I just don’t stay with it.\n\nSame with a game. I might have it on, but I’m half watching, half thinking about something else. I don’t really sit there and think, “I don’t enjoy this anymore.” It’s just… kind of hard for me to say one way or the other.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about work, family, or other tasks, do you notice that activities like fixing things or watching games feel less engaging or less interesting to you than they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can pin it down, honestly. If I’m really not thinking about work or family or the stuff waiting on me, I don’t know that I get enough of that kind of clear space to compare it.\n\nLike a game might be on, and I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m thinking about whether I left something in the truck, or some little thing reminds me of a job site from years ago, and then I’m off on that. With fixing things, I can still get pulled in for a minute if I’m trying to figure out what’s wrong, but then it branches off into other stuff.\n\nSo I guess I haven’t noticed it as “less interesting” exactly. It’s more that I don’t stay with it long enough to know.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about work, family, or other responsibilities, do you notice that activities you used to enjoy, like fixing things or watching games, feel less interesting or less pleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I hear what you’re asking, I just… I don’t know if I can give you a yes-or-no on it. I don’t really get many times where my head is that clear.\n\nLike, if I’m watching a game, I might get into it for a little bit, then I’ll notice the announcer’s voice or think about a field I worked on once that had bad drainage, and then I’m thinking about that instead. Or with fixing something, I can still like figuring out the problem, but then I’m off looking for a tool and noticing three other things that need doing.\n\nSo I’m not sure if it’s less pleasurable, exactly. I just don’t stay in it cleanly enough to tell."
